I guess the main reason is I’m just… exhausted all the time. Not just tired like I stayed up too late, but worn out in this heavy, whole-body way. I can sleep a full night and still wake up feeling like my body is made of sandbags, like everything takes so much effort before the day has even started.

It’s gotten to where getting through work feels like I’m dragging myself from one thing to the next, and I work in marketing research, so I need to think clearly and stay focused, and even that feels like climbing a hill some days. And then when I get home, something small like folding laundry or putting things away can feel completely overwhelming, like it takes everything I have. I just don’t feel like myself, and I’m tired of feeling this drained all the time.

Yeah… I have. Most days, honestly. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic like that, but I wake up and there’s this heavy, flat feeling in my chest before I’ve even really started the day. And I get this thought—not exactly in words every time, but it’s there—that there isn’t some better version of my life waiting up ahead. Like I can’t picture a future where I feel lighter or excited about things. It just feels like more of the same, and that scares me, because I don’t think I used to feel that way so much.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have, pretty noticeably. I used to be pretty good about making time for things that felt like mine, you know? Like hiking on weekends, or going to yoga, or working in my garden after work when the weather was decent. And I used to really like taking photos of birds—nothing professional, just with my camera, walking around and trying to catch them in the yard or on trails.

But lately it’s like… even thinking about doing those things feels like too much effort. I’ll tell myself I should go for a hike because I know I used to enjoy it, and then I’ll end up canceling or just not going. My yoga mat is just sitting there. The garden is kind of neglected, which makes me feel bad, because that used to calm me down. And my camera has been sitting in the closet for I don’t even know how long. I look at it and think, “I should take it out,” but then it just feels pointless somehow, like it wouldn’t be worth the energy.

It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like they don’t pull me toward them anymore. They feel far away, like things a different version of me used to care about.

My mind starts going to this really dark place where I think maybe he’d be better off without me. Yes… I think so. Or, maybe it’s not always like a dramatic crash after one specific thing, but activity definitely seems to wipe me out in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.

No, not really. I mean, I can be in bed for what should be a full night, and when I wake up it doesn’t feel like I’ve rested. It’s more like I’m just… coming back online already exhausted.

I’m not sure I’d put it as “worrying a lot more,” exactly. I mean, I do think about things—work, money, whether I’m keeping up with everything, that kind of normal life stuff—but I don’t know that it feels like this big separate thing where I’m constantly worrying across every part of my life.

I’d say it’s been about two months now, most days. It didn’t feel like it started all at once, like one morning I woke up and everything was different, but looking back, that’s about when this depressed, slowed-down, drained feeling really became the usual.

At first I think I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or that I was tired from work, or maybe I just needed a weekend to catch up. But then the weekends didn’t really reset anything, and the next week would start and I’d still feel the same. So… yeah, about the past two months is probably the clearest answer. Most days during that time I’ve felt low and just kind of exhausted in a way that feels hard to push through.

And then when I get home, something small like folding laundry or putting things away can feel completely overwhelming, like it takes everything I have. I just don’t feel like myself, and I’m tired of feeling this drained all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Most days, honestly. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic like that, but I wake up and there’s this heavy, flat feeling in my chest before I’ve even really started the day. Like I’ll sit at the kitchen table with my coffee, just staring out at the yard, and everything feels kind of colorless.\n\nAnd I get this thought—not exactly in words every time, but it’s there—that there isn’t some better version of my life waiting up ahead. Like I can’t picture a future where I feel lighter or excited about things. It just feels like more of the same, and that scares me, because I don’t think I used to feel that way so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, pretty noticeably. I used to be pretty good about making time for things that felt like mine, you know? Like hiking on weekends, or going to yoga, or working in my garden after work when the weather was decent. And I used to really like taking photos of birds—nothing professional, just with my camera, walking around and trying to catch them in the yard or on trails.\n\nBut lately it’s like… even thinking about doing those things feels like too much effort. I’ll tell myself I should go for a hike because I know I used to enjoy it, and then I’ll end up canceling or just not going. My yoga mat is just sitting there. The garden is kind of neglected, which makes me feel bad, because that used to calm me down. And my camera has been sitting in the closet for I don’t even know how long. I look at it and think, “I should take it out,” but then it just feels pointless somehow, like it wouldn’t be worth the energy.\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like they don’t pull me toward them anymore. They feel far away, like things a different version of me used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of wanting to die?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it too strongly, because that makes it feel more real, but… yes, I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s usually late at night, when everything is quiet and my husband is asleep, and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. My mind starts going to this really dark place where I think maybe he’d be better off without me. Like I’m just this weight in the house, or I’m making everything harder by being the way I am right now.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. I need to say that clearly. It’s not like I’ve sat down and figured something out. But the thought keeps coming back, and that’s what scares me. It’s not just a passing thing that happened once. It feels like it’s been showing up enough that I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times where I was sad or worn down, like anyone, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same as this, or if it lasted a full couple of weeks in that way.\n\nI’m trying to think back and it all gets a little blurry, honestly. There were stretches where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if I was actually depressed like this or if I was just stressed and tired. I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is. Or, maybe it’s not always like a dramatic crash after one specific thing, but activity definitely seems to wipe me out in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nHonestly, feeling worn out all the time is a big part of why I came in. And then if I do anything that takes effort—work especially, trying to focus through the day, answering emails, analyzing reports, sitting in meetings—it just drains me completely. By the time I’m done, I don’t feel like I’ve just worked a normal day. I feel like I’ve been wrung out.\n\nEven things at home, like folding laundry or putting dishes away, can feel like this huge production. I’ll start something small and then have to sit down, not because it’s physically hard in the usual sense, but because it feels like I just don’t have anything in the tank. It’s embarrassing to say, because these are basic things, but getting through them takes everything I have lately.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t think I’ve noticed that pattern, no. I definitely get tired after doing things, especially if I’ve already been feeling drained that day, but I’m not sure I’d call it a delayed crash.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had low periods before, like times where I felt really discouraged or kind of flat, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was depressed and losing interest in things nearly every day for two full weeks.

I mean… I’ve had times where I was sad or worn down, like anyone, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same as this, or if it lasted a full couple of weeks in that way.\n\nI’m trying to think back and it all gets a little blurry, honestly. There were stretches where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if I was actually depressed like this or if I was just stressed and tired. I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is. This current stretch feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s settled in.\n\nDoctor: After physical or mental activity, do you tend to feel markedly worse afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Or, maybe it’s not always like a dramatic crash after one specific thing, but activity definitely seems to wipe me out in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nHonestly, feeling worn out all the time is a big part of why I came in. Even if I’ve been in bed for a full night, I’ll get up and it’s like my body is made of sandbags. Like everything is heavy before the day has even started. And then if I do anything that takes effort—work especially, trying to focus through the day, answering emails, analyzing reports, sitting in meetings—it just drains me completely. By the time I’m done, I don’t feel like I’ve just worked a normal day. I feel like I’ve been wrung out.\n\nEven things at home, like folding laundry or putting dishes away, can feel like this huge production. I’ll start something small and then have to sit down, not because it’s physically hard in the usual sense, but because it feels like I just don’t have anything in the tank. It’s embarrassing to say, because these are basic things, but getting through them takes everything I have lately.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I can be in bed for what should be a full night, and when I wake up it doesn’t feel like I’ve rested. It’s more like I’m just… coming back online already exhausted.\n\nMy body feels so heavy in the morning, like I’m made of sandbags or something. I’ll lie there thinking about getting up, and even that feels like it takes effort. And then once I do get moving, it’s not like I shake it off after coffee or a shower. It kind of follows me through the day.\n\nThat’s honestly a big reason I came in. I feel worn out all the time. Getting through work takes everything out of me, and then even simple things at home, like folding laundry, feel like this huge task. So no, I wouldn’t say I wake up refreshed. I wake up feeling like I’m already behind on energy before I’ve even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been unusually sensitive to cold lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed anything unusual like that.\n\nI do get cold sometimes, but I think it’s more normal stuff, like if the office air conditioning is too high or I’m sitting still for a while. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “Wow, why am I freezing when everyone else is fine?” At least not that I can clearly remember.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually sensitive to cold lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot more than usual about different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worrying a lot more,” exactly. I mean, I do think about things—work, money, whether I’m keeping up with everything, that kind of normal life stuff—but I don’t know that it feels like this big separate thing where I’m constantly worrying across every part of my life.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice when I’m already worn down, my thoughts can get more repetitive, like I’ll go over what I still need to do or whether I’m falling behind. But I’m not sure if that’s worry in the way you mean, or just me trying to keep track of everything and feeling like I don’t have enough bandwidth.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe a little at times, but I haven’t really noticed a major increase in worrying about lots of different areas.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed and drained most days?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now, most days. It didn’t feel like it started all at once, like one morning I woke up and everything was different, but looking back, that’s about when this depressed, slowed-down, drained feeling really became the usual.\n\nAt first I think I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or that I was tired from work, or maybe I just needed a weekend to catch up. But then the weekends didn’t really reset anything, and the next week would start and I’d still feel the same. So… yeah, about the past two months is probably the clearest answer. Most days during that time I’ve felt low and just kind of exhausted in a way that feels hard to push through.\n\nDoctor: After even mild physical or mental activity, do you often have a delayed crash where your exhaustion gets clearly worse and lasts into the next day or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that pattern, no. I definitely get tired after doing things, especially if I’ve already been feeling drained that day, but I’m not sure I’d call it a delayed crash.\n\nLike, if I have a busy workday or I have to concentrate for a long stretch, I might feel more worn out afterward, but it seems more immediate or just part of the same general exhaustion. I can’t really point to a clear thing where I do something mild—like a short errand or a bit of mental effort—and then the next day I’m dramatically worse because of that specific activity.\n\nI guess it’s hard to separate because I’ve already been feeling low energy most days, so sometimes the next day is still bad, but I don’t know that it’s a delayed reaction to activity. I haven’t been tracking it that closely, though.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed and lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had low periods before, like times where I felt really discouraged or kind of flat, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was depressed and losing interest in things nearly every day for two full weeks.\n\nPart of the problem is I don’t think I tracked it that way at the time. I’d usually just think, “I’m stressed,” or “I’m in a rut,” or “I need to get through this week,” and then I’d sort of keep going. Looking back, some stretches probably felt heavy, but I’m not sure whether they met that kind of consistent, almost every day pattern you’re asking about.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I don’t know. I definitely get tired after doing things, especially if I’ve already been feeling drained that day, but I’m not sure I’d call it a delayed crash.\n\nLike, if I have a busy workday or I have to concentrate for a long stretch, I might feel more worn out afterward, but it seems more immediate or just part of the same general exhaustion. I can’t really point to a clear thing where I do something mild—like a short errand or a bit of mental effort—and then the next day I’m dramatically worse because of that specific activity.\n\nI guess it’s hard to separate because I’ve already been feeling low energy most days, so sometimes the next day is still bad, but I don’t know that it’s a delayed reaction to activity. I haven’t been tracking it that closely, though.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed and lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had low periods before, like times where I felt really discouraged or kind of flat, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was depressed and losing interest in things nearly every day for two full weeks.\n\nPart of the problem is I don’t think I tracked it that way at the time. I’d usually just think, “I’m stressed,” or “I’m in a rut,” or “I need to get through this week,” and then I’d sort of keep going. Looking back, some stretches probably felt heavy, but I’m not sure whether they met that kind of consistent, almost every day pattern you’re asking about.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I don’t know. I can’t point to a clear earlier episode the way I can with these past couple of months.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave other people commented that you seem to be moving or speaking more slowly lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 43 / market_research_analyst_or_marketing_specialist / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"My husband has pointed out that I move around the house like I am underwater, and when he asks me something simple it can take me a while to answer because the words come out so slowly.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… my husband has noticed it, actually. He’s said a few times that I seem like I’m moving around the house like I’m underwater, which was kind of hard to hear because I didn’t realize it looked that obvious from the outside.

And with talking, too—he’ll ask me something really simple, like where something is or what I want to do about dinner, and I can feel there’s this lag. Like I know I should answer, but it takes me a while to get the words together, and then when I do answer, they come out slowly. He’s pointed that out more than once lately.

I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m sorry, I’m probably circling around it because I don’t have a clean answer.

I can’t really identify a definite earlier period where I’d say, yes, I was depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks. Like I’ll sit at the kitchen table with my coffee, just staring out at the yard, and everything feels kind of colorless.\n\nAnd I get this thought—not exactly in words every time, but it’s there—that there isn’t some better version of my life waiting up ahead. Like I can’t picture a future where I feel lighter or excited about things. It just feels like more of the same, and that scares me, because I don’t think I used to feel that way so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, pretty noticeably. I used to be pretty good about making time for things that felt like mine, you know? Like hiking on weekends, or going to yoga, or working in my garden after work when the weather was decent. And I used to really like taking photos of birds—nothing professional, just with my camera, walking around and trying to catch them in the yard or on trails.\n\nBut lately it’s like… even thinking about doing those things feels like too much effort. I’ll tell myself I should go for a hike because I know I used to enjoy it, and then I’ll end up canceling or just not going. My yoga mat is just sitting there. The garden is kind of neglected, which makes me feel bad, because that used to calm me down. And my camera has been sitting in the closet for I don’t even know how long. I look at it and think, “I should take it out,” but then it just feels pointless somehow, like it wouldn’t be worth the energy.\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like they don’t pull me toward them anymore. They feel far away, like things a different version of me used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of wanting to die?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it too strongly, because that makes it feel more real, but… yes, I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s usually late at night, when everything is quiet and my husband is asleep, and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. My mind starts going to this really dark place where I think maybe he’d be better off without me. Like I’m just this weight in the house, or I’m making everything harder by being the way I am right now.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. I need to say that clearly. It’s not like I’ve sat down and figured something out. But the thought keeps coming back, and that’s what scares me. It’s not just a passing thing that happened once. It feels like it’s been showing up enough that I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times where I was sad or worn down, like anyone, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same as this, or if it lasted a full couple of weeks in that way.\n\nI’m trying to think back and it all gets a little blurry, honestly. There were stretches where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if I was actually depressed like this or if I was just stressed and tired. I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is. This current stretch feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s settled in.\n\nDoctor: After physical or mental activity, do you tend to feel markedly worse afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Or, maybe it’s not always like a dramatic crash after one specific thing, but activity definitely seems to wipe me out in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nHonestly, feeling worn out all the time is a big part of why I came in. Even if I’ve been in bed for a full night, I’ll get up and it’s like my body is made of sandbags. Like everything is heavy before the day has even started. And then if I do anything that takes effort—work especially, trying to focus through the day, answering emails, analyzing reports, sitting in meetings—it just drains me completely. By the time I’m done, I don’t feel like I’ve just worked a normal day. I feel like I’ve been wrung out.\n\nEven things at home, like folding laundry or putting dishes away, can feel like this huge production. I’ll start something small and then have to sit down, not because it’s physically hard in the usual sense, but because it feels like I just don’t have anything in the tank. It’s embarrassing to say, because these are basic things, but getting through them takes everything I have lately.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I can be in bed for what should be a full night, and when I wake up it doesn’t feel like I’ve rested. It’s more like I’m just… coming back online already exhausted.\n\nMy body feels so heavy in the morning, like I’m made of sandbags or something. I’ll lie there thinking about getting up, and even that feels like it takes effort. And then once I do get moving, it’s not like I shake it off after coffee or a shower. It kind of follows me through the day.\n\nThat’s honestly a big reason I came in. I feel worn out all the time. Getting through work takes everything out of me, and then even simple things at home, like folding laundry, feel like this huge task. So no, I wouldn’t say I wake up refreshed. I wake up feeling like I’m already behind on energy before I’ve even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been unusually sensitive to cold lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed anything unusual like that.\n\nI do get cold sometimes, but I think it’s more normal stuff, like if the office air conditioning is too high or I’m sitting still for a while. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “Wow, why am I freezing when everyone else is fine?” At least not that I can clearly remember.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually sensitive to cold lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot more than usual about different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worrying a lot more,” exactly. I mean, I do think about things—work, money, whether I’m keeping up with everything, that kind of normal life stuff—but I don’t know that it feels like this big separate thing where I’m constantly worrying across every part of my life.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice when I’m already worn down, my thoughts can get more repetitive, like I’ll go over what I still need to do or whether I’m falling behind. But I’m not sure if that’s worry in the way you mean, or just me trying to keep track of everything and feeling like I don’t have enough bandwidth.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe a little at times, but I haven’t really noticed a major increase in worrying about lots of different areas.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed and drained most days?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now, most days. It didn’t feel like it started all at once, like one morning I woke up and everything was different, but looking back, that’s about when this depressed, slowed-down, drained feeling really became the usual.\n\nAt first I think I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or that I was tired from work, or maybe I just needed a weekend to catch up. But then the weekends didn’t really reset anything, and the next week would start and I’d still feel the same. So… yeah, about the past two months is probably the clearest answer. Most days during that time I’ve felt low and just kind of exhausted in a way that feels hard to push through.\n\nDoctor: After even mild physical or mental activity, do you often have a delayed crash where your exhaustion gets clearly worse and lasts into the next day or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that pattern, no. I definitely get tired after doing things, especially if I’ve already been feeling drained that day, but I’m not sure I’d call it a delayed crash.\n\nLike, if I have a busy workday or I have to concentrate for a long stretch, I might feel more worn out afterward, but it seems more immediate or just part of the same general exhaustion. I look at it and think, “I should take it out,” but then it just feels pointless somehow, like it wouldn’t be worth the energy.\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like they don’t pull me toward them anymore. They feel far away, like things a different version of me used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of wanting to die?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it too strongly, because that makes it feel more real, but… yes, I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s usually late at night, when everything is quiet and my husband is asleep, and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. My mind starts going to this really dark place where I think maybe he’d be better off without me. Like I’m just this weight in the house, or I’m making everything harder by being the way I am right now.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. I need to say that clearly. It’s not like I’ve sat down and figured something out. But the thought keeps coming back, and that’s what scares me. It’s not just a passing thing that happened once. It feels like it’s been showing up enough that I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times where I was sad or worn down, like anyone, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same as this, or if it lasted a full couple of weeks in that way.\n\nI’m trying to think back and it all gets a little blurry, honestly. There were stretches where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if I was actually depressed like this or if I was just stressed and tired. I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is. This current stretch feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s settled in.\n\nDoctor: After physical or mental activity, do you tend to feel markedly worse afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Or, maybe it’s not always like a dramatic crash after one specific thing, but activity definitely seems to wipe me out in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nHonestly, feeling worn out all the time is a big part of why I came in. Even if I’ve been in bed for a full night, I’ll get up and it’s like my body is made of sandbags. Like everything is heavy before the day has even started. And then if I do anything that takes effort—work especially, trying to focus through the day, answering emails, analyzing reports, sitting in meetings—it just drains me completely. By the time I’m done, I don’t feel like I’ve just worked a normal day. I feel like I’ve been wrung out.\n\nEven things at home, like folding laundry or putting dishes away, can feel like this huge production. I’ll start something small and then have to sit down, not because it’s physically hard in the usual sense, but because it feels like I just don’t have anything in the tank. It’s embarrassing to say, because these are basic things, but getting through them takes everything I have lately.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I can be in bed for what should be a full night, and when I wake up it doesn’t feel like I’ve rested. It’s more like I’m just… coming back online already exhausted.\n\nMy body feels so heavy in the morning, like I’m made of sandbags or something. I’ll lie there thinking about getting up, and even that feels like it takes effort. And then once I do get moving, it’s not like I shake it off after coffee or a shower. It kind of follows me through the day.\n\nThat’s honestly a big reason I came in. I feel worn out all the time. Getting through work takes everything out of me, and then even simple things at home, like folding laundry, feel like this huge task. So no, I wouldn’t say I wake up refreshed. I wake up feeling like I’m already behind on energy before I’ve even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been unusually sensitive to cold lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed anything unusual like that.\n\nI do get cold sometimes, but I think it’s more normal stuff, like if the office air conditioning is too high or I’m sitting still for a while. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “Wow, why am I freezing when everyone else is fine?” At least not that I can clearly remember.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually sensitive to cold lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot more than usual about different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worrying a lot more,” exactly. I mean, I do think about things—work, money, whether I’m keeping up with everything, that kind of normal life stuff—but I don’t know that it feels like this big separate thing where I’m constantly worrying across every part of my life.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice when I’m already worn down, my thoughts can get more repetitive, like I’ll go over what I still need to do or whether I’m falling behind. But I’m not sure if that’s worry in the way you mean, or just me trying to keep track of everything and feeling like I don’t have enough bandwidth.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe a little at times, but I haven’t really noticed a major increase in worrying about lots of different areas.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed and drained most days?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now, most days. It didn’t feel like it started all at once, like one morning I woke up and everything was different, but looking back, that’s about when this depressed, slowed-down, drained feeling really became the usual.\n\nAt first I think I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or that I was tired from work, or maybe I just needed a weekend to catch up. But then the weekends didn’t really reset anything, and the next week would start and I’d still feel the same. So… yeah, about the past two months is probably the clearest answer. Most days during that time I’ve felt low and just kind of exhausted in a way that feels hard to push through.\n\nDoctor: After even mild physical or mental activity, do you often have a delayed crash where your exhaustion gets clearly worse and lasts into the next day or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that pattern, no. I definitely get tired after doing things, especially if I’ve already been feeling drained that day, but I’m not sure I’d call it a delayed crash.\n\nLike, if I have a busy workday or I have to concentrate for a long stretch, I might feel more worn out afterward, but it seems more immediate or just part of the same general exhaustion. I can’t really point to a clear thing where I do something mild—like a short errand or a bit of mental effort—and then the next day I’m dramatically worse because of that specific activity.\n\nI guess it’s hard to separate because I’ve already been feeling low energy most days, so sometimes the next day is still bad, but I don’t know that it’s a delayed reaction to activity. I haven’t been tracking it that closely, though.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed and lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had low periods before, like times where I felt really discouraged or kind of flat, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was depressed and losing interest in things nearly every day for two full weeks.\n\nPart of the problem is I don’t think I tracked it that way at the time. I’d usually just think, “I’m stressed,” or “I’m in a rut,” or “I need to get through this week,” and then I’d sort of keep going. Looking back, some stretches probably felt heavy, but I’m not sure whether they met that kind of consistent, almost every day pattern you’re asking about.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I don’t know. I can’t point to a clear earlier episode the way I can with these past couple of months.\n\nDoctor: Have other people commented that you seem to be moving or speaking more slowly lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my husband has noticed it, actually. He’s said a few times that I seem like I’m moving around the house like I’m underwater, which was kind of hard to hear because I didn’t realize it looked that obvious from the outside.\n\nAnd with talking, too—he’ll ask me something really simple, like where something is or what I want to do about dinner, and I can feel there’s this lag. Like I know I should answer, but it takes me a while to get the words together, and then when I do answer, they come out slowly. He’s pointed that out more than once lately.\n\nDoctor: At any time before these past two months, was there ever a period when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m sorry, I’m probably circling around it because I don’t have a clean answer.\n\nI can’t really identify a definite earlier period where I’d say, yes, I was depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks. I’ve had rough patches before, definitely—times where I felt worn down or unmotivated or like I was just pushing through—but when I try to look back and apply that “nearly every day for two weeks” standard, it gets fuzzy.\n\nAt the time, I don’t think I would have described it that way. I probably would’ve said I was stressed, or tired, or overwhelmed. And I wasn’t keeping track of whether it was every day or how long it lasted. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing. I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure, but I can’t point to a clear episode before these last couple of months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’d say I’ve been sleeping more, exactly.\n\nIt’s a little hard to judge because my schedule has felt kind of blurred lately, and I’m not always great at keeping track of the hours. Some days I might stay in bed longer, but I don’t know if that means I’m actually sleeping more or just… not getting up right away. So if you mean actual hours asleep, I haven’t clearly noticed that increasing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Most days during that time I’ve felt low and just kind of exhausted in a way that feels hard to push through.\n\nDoctor: After even mild physical or mental activity, do you often have a delayed crash where your exhaustion gets clearly worse and lasts into the next day or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that pattern, no. I wake up feeling like I’m already behind on energy before I’ve even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been unusually sensitive to cold lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “Wow, why am I freezing when everyone else is fine?” At least not that I can clearly remember.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually sensitive to cold lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot more than usual about different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worrying a lot more,” exactly. I mean, I do think about things—work, money, whether I’m keeping up with everything, that kind of normal life stuff—but I don’t know that it feels like this big separate thing where I’m constantly worrying across every part of my life.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice when I’m already worn down, my thoughts can get more repetitive, like I’ll go over what I still need to do or whether I’m falling behind. But I’m not sure if that’s worry in the way you mean, or just me trying to keep track of everything and feeling like I don’t have enough bandwidth.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe a little at times, but I haven’t really noticed a major increase in worrying about lots of different areas.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed and drained most days?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now, most days. It didn’t feel like it started all at once, like one morning I woke up and everything was different, but looking back, that’s about when this depressed, slowed-down, drained feeling really became the usual.\n\nAt first I think I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or that I was tired from work, or maybe I just needed a weekend to catch up. But then the weekends didn’t really reset anything, and the next week would start and I’d still feel the same. So… yeah, about the past two months is probably the clearest answer. Most days during that time I’ve felt low and just kind of exhausted in a way that feels hard to push through.\n\nDoctor: After even mild physical or mental activity, do you often have a delayed crash where your exhaustion gets clearly worse and lasts into the next day or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that pattern, no. I definitely get tired after doing things, especially if I’ve already been feeling drained that day, but I’m not sure I’d call it a delayed crash.\n\nLike, if I have a busy workday or I have to concentrate for a long stretch, I might feel more worn out afterward, but it seems more immediate or just part of the same general exhaustion. I can’t really point to a clear thing where I do something mild—like a short errand or a bit of mental effort—and then the next day I’m dramatically worse because of that specific activity.\n\nI guess it’s hard to separate because I’ve already been feeling low energy most days, so sometimes the next day is still bad, but I don’t know that it’s a delayed reaction to activity. I haven’t been tracking it that closely, though.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed and lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had low periods before, like times where I felt really discouraged or kind of flat, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was depressed and losing interest in things nearly every day for two full weeks.\n\nPart of the problem is I don’t think I tracked it that way at the time. I’d usually just think, “I’m stressed,” or “I’m in a rut,” or “I need to get through this week,” and then I’d sort of keep going. I can’t point to a clear earlier episode the way I can with these past couple of months.\n\nDoctor: Have other people commented that you seem to be moving or speaking more slowly lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my husband has noticed it, actually. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, “worthless” feels like a strong word, but I do keep getting stuck in this loop where I feel like I’ve done something really wrong.

It’s mostly about the mortgage decision. I keep replaying it over and over in my head, like I’m reviewing evidence against myself. My husband keeps reminding me that we made that decision together, that it wasn’t just me, and logically I know that. I can say the words. But it doesn’t really land. I still feel like I should have known better, or protected us somehow. I keep thinking, if something goes wrong, it’ll be my fault. So yes, there’s definitely guilt. More than makes sense, probably.

There were stretches where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if I was actually depressed like this or if I was just stressed and tired. I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is. This current stretch feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s settled in.\n\nDoctor: After physical or mental activity, do you tend to feel markedly worse afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Or, maybe it’s not always like a dramatic crash after one specific thing, but activity definitely seems to wipe me out in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nHonestly, feeling worn out all the time is a big part of why I came in. Even if I’ve been in bed for a full night, I’ll get up and it’s like my body is made of sandbags. Like everything is heavy before the day has even started. And then if I do anything that takes effort—work especially, trying to focus through the day, answering emails, analyzing reports, sitting in meetings—it just drains me completely. By the time I’m done, I don’t feel like I’ve just worked a normal day. I feel like I’ve been wrung out.\n\nEven things at home, like folding laundry or putting dishes away, can feel like this huge production. I’ll start something small and then have to sit down, not because it’s physically hard in the usual sense, but because it feels like I just don’t have anything in the tank. It’s embarrassing to say, because these are basic things, but getting through them takes everything I have lately.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I can be in bed for what should be a full night, and when I wake up it doesn’t feel like I’ve rested. It’s more like I’m just… coming back online already exhausted.\n\nMy body feels so heavy in the morning, like I’m made of sandbags or something. I’ll lie there thinking about getting up, and even that feels like it takes effort. And then once I do get moving, it’s not like I shake it off after coffee or a shower. It kind of follows me through the day.\n\nThat’s honestly a big reason I came in. I feel worn out all the time. Getting through work takes everything out of me, and then even simple things at home, like folding laundry, feel like this huge task. So no, I wouldn’t say I wake up refreshed. I wake up feeling like I’m already behind on energy before I’ve even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been unusually sensitive to cold lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed anything unusual like that.\n\nI do get cold sometimes, but I think it’s more normal stuff, like if the office air conditioning is too high or I’m sitting still for a while. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “Wow, why am I freezing when everyone else is fine?” At least not that I can clearly remember.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually sensitive to cold lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot more than usual about different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worrying a lot more,” exactly. I mean, I do think about things—work, money, whether I’m keeping up with everything, that kind of normal life stuff—but I don’t know that it feels like this big separate thing where I’m constantly worrying across every part of my life.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice when I’m already worn down, my thoughts can get more repetitive, like I’ll go over what I still need to do or whether I’m falling behind. But I’m not sure if that’s worry in the way you mean, or just me trying to keep track of everything and feeling like I don’t have enough bandwidth.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe a little at times, but I haven’t really noticed a major increase in worrying about lots of different areas.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed and drained most days?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now, most days. It didn’t feel like it started all at once, like one morning I woke up and everything was different, but looking back, that’s about when this depressed, slowed-down, drained feeling really became the usual.\n\nAt first I think I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or that I was tired from work, or maybe I just needed a weekend to catch up. But then the weekends didn’t really reset anything, and the next week would start and I’d still feel the same. So… yeah, about the past two months is probably the clearest answer. Most days during that time I’ve felt low and just kind of exhausted in a way that feels hard to push through.\n\nDoctor: After even mild physical or mental activity, do you often have a delayed crash where your exhaustion gets clearly worse and lasts into the next day or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that pattern, no. I definitely get tired after doing things, especially if I’ve already been feeling drained that day, but I’m not sure I’d call it a delayed crash.\n\nLike, if I have a busy workday or I have to concentrate for a long stretch, I might feel more worn out afterward, but it seems more immediate or just part of the same general exhaustion. I haven’t been tracking it that closely, though.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed and lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like I can’t picture a future where I feel lighter or excited about things. It just feels like more of the same, and that scares me, because I don’t think I used to feel that way so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, pretty noticeably. I used to be pretty good about making time for things that felt like mine, you know? Like hiking on weekends, or going to yoga, or working in my garden after work when the weather was decent. And I used to really like taking photos of birds—nothing professional, just with my camera, walking around and trying to catch them in the yard or on trails.\n\nBut lately it’s like… even thinking about doing those things feels like too much effort. I’ll tell myself I should go for a hike because I know I used to enjoy it, and then I’ll end up canceling or just not going. My yoga mat is just sitting there. The garden is kind of neglected, which makes me feel bad, because that used to calm me down. And my camera has been sitting in the closet for I don’t even know how long. I look at it and think, “I should take it out,” but then it just feels pointless somehow, like it wouldn’t be worth the energy.\n\nIt’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like they don’t pull me toward them anymore. They feel far away, like things a different version of me used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of wanting to die?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it too strongly, because that makes it feel more real, but… yes, I’ve had thoughts like that.\n\nIt’s usually late at night, when everything is quiet and my husband is asleep, and I’m just lying there staring at the ceiling. My mind starts going to this really dark place where I think maybe he’d be better off without me. Like I’m just this weight in the house, or I’m making everything harder by being the way I am right now.\n\nI haven’t made a plan or anything like that. I need to say that clearly. It’s not like I’ve sat down and figured something out. But the thought keeps coming back, and that’s what scares me. It’s not just a passing thing that happened once. It feels like it’s been showing up enough that I don’t feel like I can just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Before this current stretch, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for a couple of weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve had times where I was sad or worn down, like anyone, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it the same as this, or if it lasted a full couple of weeks in that way.\n\nI’m trying to think back and it all gets a little blurry, honestly. There were stretches where I felt low or kind of shut down, but I don’t know if I was actually depressed like this or if I was just stressed and tired. I don’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is. This current stretch feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s settled in.\n\nDoctor: After physical or mental activity, do you tend to feel markedly worse afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Or, maybe it’s not always like a dramatic crash after one specific thing, but activity definitely seems to wipe me out in a way that doesn’t feel normal for me.\n\nHonestly, feeling worn out all the time is a big part of why I came in. Even if I’ve been in bed for a full night, I’ll get up and it’s like my body is made of sandbags. Like everything is heavy before the day has even started. And then if I do anything that takes effort—work especially, trying to focus through the day, answering emails, analyzing reports, sitting in meetings—it just drains me completely. By the time I’m done, I don’t feel like I’ve just worked a normal day. I feel like I’ve been wrung out.\n\nEven things at home, like folding laundry or putting dishes away, can feel like this huge production. I’ll start something small and then have to sit down, not because it’s physically hard in the usual sense, but because it feels like I just don’t have anything in the tank. It’s embarrassing to say, because these are basic things, but getting through them takes everything I have lately.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, do you usually feel refreshed?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I can be in bed for what should be a full night, and when I wake up it doesn’t feel like I’ve rested. It’s more like I’m just… coming back online already exhausted.\n\nMy body feels so heavy in the morning, like I’m made of sandbags or something. I’ll lie there thinking about getting up, and even that feels like it takes effort. And then once I do get moving, it’s not like I shake it off after coffee or a shower. It kind of follows me through the day.\n\nThat’s honestly a big reason I came in. I feel worn out all the time. Getting through work takes everything out of me, and then even simple things at home, like folding laundry, feel like this huge task. So no, I wouldn’t say I wake up refreshed. I wake up feeling like I’m already behind on energy before I’ve even started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been unusually sensitive to cold lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t really noticed anything unusual like that.\n\nI do get cold sometimes, but I think it’s more normal stuff, like if the office air conditioning is too high or I’m sitting still for a while. I haven’t had a moment where I thought, “Wow, why am I freezing when everyone else is fine?” At least not that I can clearly remember.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually sensitive to cold lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying a lot more than usual about different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worrying a lot more,” exactly. I mean, I do think about things—work, money, whether I’m keeping up with everything, that kind of normal life stuff—but I don’t know that it feels like this big separate thing where I’m constantly worrying across every part of my life.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice when I’m already worn down, my thoughts can get more repetitive, like I’ll go over what I still need to do or whether I’m falling behind. But I’m not sure if that’s worry in the way you mean, or just me trying to keep track of everything and feeling like I don’t have enough bandwidth.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe a little at times, but I haven’t really noticed a major increase in worrying about lots of different areas.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this depressed and drained most days?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months now, most days. It didn’t feel like it started all at once, like one morning I woke up and everything was different, but looking back, that’s about when this depressed, slowed-down, drained feeling really became the usual.\n\nAt first I think I kept telling myself it was just a rough week, or that I was tired from work, or maybe I just needed a weekend to catch up. But then the weekends didn’t really reset anything, and the next week would start and I’d still feel the same. So… yeah, about the past two months is probably the clearest answer. Most days during that time I’ve felt low and just kind of exhausted in a way that feels hard to push through.\n\nDoctor: After even mild physical or mental activity, do you often have a delayed crash where your exhaustion gets clearly worse and lasts into the next day or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that pattern, no. I definitely get tired after doing things, especially if I’ve already been feeling drained that day, but I’m not sure I’d call it a delayed crash.\n\nLike, if I have a busy workday or I have to concentrate for a long stretch, I might feel more worn out afterward, but it seems more immediate or just part of the same general exhaustion. I can’t really point to a clear thing where I do something mild—like a short errand or a bit of mental effort—and then the next day I’m dramatically worse because of that specific activity.\n\nI guess it’s hard to separate because I’ve already been feeling low energy most days, so sometimes the next day is still bad, but I don’t know that it’s a delayed reaction to activity. I haven’t been tracking it that closely, though.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months, was there ever another time in your life when you felt depressed and lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve had low periods before, like times where I felt really discouraged or kind of flat, but I don’t know if I can confidently say it was depressed and losing interest in things nearly every day for two full weeks.\n\nPart of the problem is I don’t think I tracked it that way at the time. I’d usually just think, “I’m stressed,” or “I’m in a rut,” or “I need to get through this week,” and then I’d sort of keep going. Looking back, some stretches probably felt heavy, but I’m not sure whether they met that kind of consistent, almost every day pattern you’re asking about.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I don’t know. I can’t point to a clear earlier episode the way I can with these past couple of months.\n\nDoctor: Have other people commented that you seem to be moving or speaking more slowly lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my husband has noticed it, actually. He’s said a few times that I seem like I’m moving around the house like I’m underwater, which was kind of hard to hear because I didn’t realize it looked that obvious from the outside.\n\nAnd with talking, too—he’ll ask me something really simple, like where something is or what I want to do about dinner, and I can feel there’s this lag. Like I know I should answer, but it takes me a while to get the words together, and then when I do answer, they come out slowly. He’s pointed that out more than once lately.\n\nDoctor: At any time before these past two months, was there ever a period when you felt depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I know you asked that a minute ago, and I’m sorry, I’m probably circling around it because I don’t have a clean answer.\n\nI can’t really identify a definite earlier period where I’d say, yes, I was depressed or lost interest in things nearly every day for at least two weeks. I’ve had rough patches before, definitely—times where I felt worn down or unmotivated or like I was just pushing through—but when I try to look back and apply that “nearly every day for two weeks” standard, it gets fuzzy.\n\nAt the time, I don’t think I would have described it that way. I probably would’ve said I was stressed, or tired, or overwhelmed. And I wasn’t keeping track of whether it was every day or how long it lasted. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m just guessing. I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure, but I can’t point to a clear episode before these last couple of months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been sleeping more than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I’d say I’ve been sleeping more, exactly.\n\nIt’s a little hard to judge because my schedule has felt kind of blurred lately, and I’m not always great at keeping track of the hours. Some days I might stay in bed longer, but I don’t know if that means I’m actually sleeping more or just… not getting up right away. So if you mean actual hours asleep, I haven’t clearly noticed that increasing.\n\nDoctor: Have you gained weight مؤخراً without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, not that I’ve noticed in any clear way.\n\nI haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t give you a number. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything like that. I mean, some days I feel kind of uncomfortable in my body, but I don’t know if that’s actual weight gain or just how I’m feeling.
